### [Our response to Séb Krier on Plan A](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RPgHythvMKh6eG9pS/our-response-to-seb-krier-on-plan-a)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://x.com/sebkrier/status/2075740272860811570"><span>This criticism</span></a><span> of AI 2040: Plan A by Séb Krier unfortunately seriously mischaracterizes our proposal. It also mostly contains flat assertions, not real argumentation, and the argumentation in it seems quite weak. While we appreciate constructive criticisms of Plan A, such as the ones by </span><a href="https://newsletter.forethought.org/p/plan-as-problem-with-dry-tinder"><span>Tom Davidson</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/selective-optimism-a-critique-of"><span>Richard Ngo</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://1a3orn.com/sub/2026-some-scenarios.html"><span>1a3orn</span></a><span>, we feel the need to correct the issues in Séb’s response. First, we’ll go over the specific false representations, and then we’ll give a point-by-point response.</span></p><h2><span>False Representations  </span></h2><blockquote><p><span>I’m not claiming you shouldn’t prepare and improvise in the dark, but rather that this version of preparing bakes in too much and leaves little space for the effective but uncomfortable trial-and-effort that real life requires.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>The exact opposite is true. Plan A is extremely iterative. In the status quo, there is trial and error, but ultimately companies aren’t going to choose the safer or more societally beneficial path, they are going to choose what the market wants. In Plan A there is much more time for AI companies to gain evidence and for governments to respond reasonably to the sweeping changes. Thanks to total transparency and broad deployment, all of this evidence is accessible to academics, independent researchers, and the public instead of being sealed away in the labs where only lab insiders can see it. Our plan </span><i><span>maximizes</span></i><span> learning and room to experiment.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>I think it gets a lot of economics wrong. It reifies a number of views common amongst Bay Area technologists, which include (a) extremely fast diffusion and societal transformation, (b) a view that all profits accrue maximally to the labs, (c) that the prescriptions advanced in the essay (like expropriations and forced IP diffusion) have minimal impacts on said profits; (d) the claim that you get explosive GDP growth very soon, (e) that ‘de facto’ nationalisation and profit redistribution through UBI is an optimal response.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Much of this is wrong. Specifically, we believe: (b) not all profits will accrue to labs,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref41ee8vw5548"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn41ee8vw5548">[1]</a></sup></span><span> (c) that Plan A would significantly decrease lab profits,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefa3vjcm5i5v8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fna3vjcm5i5v8">[2]</a></sup></span><span> and (e) ‘de facto’ nationalization is not an optimal response.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzynhk4ixfwe"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzynhk4ixfwe">[3]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><blockquote><p><span>The authors themselves note that </span><i><span>“there isn’t a central planner with authority to regulate AI worldwide</span></i><span>” as a fundamental issue with the status quo.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>This is an out of context quotation to make us sound bad. You can see this quotation </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/?r=tw&amp;choices=plan-a-root#examples-of-problemsissues-that-still-remain:~:text=Principle%202%3A%20Total%20Research%20Transparency"><span>in context here</span></a><span>. We note the absence of a global central planner </span><i><span>as a constraint Plan A must work within</span></i><span>. The Deal we propose does </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> create such a central planner, nor do we think this is a fundamental issue with the status quo.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>A cadre of elites decides which research directions are permissible, caps global compute and robotics, and creates state-administered scarcity rents.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>In Plan A, American and Chinese regulators do coordinate to ban egregiously dangerous research directions. But because of total research transparency (TRT), all of the evidence that informs the decision to ban a new training method is public. Anyone can inspect the evidence and check the regulators' work.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>It feels like the same plans as I’ve been hearing about for nearly a decade in the AI safety community, but filled with more details. In 2018 when I worked for the UK government, a prominent AI safety research organisation told me that “</span><i><span>we need to solve the technical alignment problem, and then simply hand it to the UN to implement everywhere</span></i><span>.” This was before the field invested in governance and politics; Plan A broadly similar, but with all sorts of mechanisms to fill in the gaps.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>The one-sentence policy proposal Séb recalls hearing eight years ago is not at all what we are calling for. In our scenario, private AI companies iteratively solve the alignment problem while regulators set a low floor by banning the most obviously dangerous AI R&amp;D practices. No one alignment plan is forced on all the companies from above.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Apparently it’s ‘surprisingly easy’ for the rest of the world to get buy-in for a technological slowdown because the only three possible outcomes of AGI development are “</span><i><span>take our jobs,</span></i><span>” “</span><i><span>cement hegemony forever</span></i><span>” and “</span><i><span>get everyone killed.</span></i><span>”</span></p></blockquote><p><span>See this quotation </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/?r=tw&amp;choices=plan-a-root#examples-of-problemsissues-that-still-remain:~:text=Step%203%3A%20Get%20Worldwide%20Buy%2Din"><span>in context</span></a><span>. We do not say, as is implied here, that these three possible outcomes are exhaustive. The whole point of writing Plan A was to increase the chance of a better outcome, namely a peaceful, multipolar world where the benefits of AI are shared widely.</span></p><h2><span>Point-by-point response</span></h2><p><span>Overall the response is light on arguments and heavy on sneering. (We’ll point out examples below.) That said, it’s not all like that. There are some substantive objections and arguments made. So we’ll now go through and respond to everything.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>The kind of world a particular AI safety milieu reaches for when asked to imagine a positive future really illustrates the extent of the field's monoculture problem.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>We think this understates the originality of Plan A. Our policy recommendations are certainly not the same thing you've been hearing from the AI safety milieu since 2018. For instance, where else have you heard someone seriously propose Total Research Transparency?</span></p><blockquote><p><span>But I'm going to take it at face value, and offer nine issues I have with this administrative utopia.</span></p><p><b><span>First</span></b><span>, I dislike scenario/table-top type fictional writing because (a) they fix some assumptions in time, so you have to basically operate within the framework and pathways offered. Dean made this point previously about an essay on the ‘permanent underclass’. It’s actually not very hard to design a scenario that implicitly favours a particular outcome. And (b) they blur the descriptive, predictive, and normative statements into one big story, and the level of detail can create a false sense of coherence. Richard Ngo</span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/selective-optimism-a-critique-of"><span> </span></a><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/selective-optimism-a-critique-of"><span>makes</span></a><span> this case well and Fleetingbits</span><a href="https://x.com/fleetingbits/status/2075276727715254546"><span> </span></a><a href="https://x.com/fleetingbits/status/2075276727715254546"><span>does</span></a><span> so too with regards to their previous 2027 piece: a lot of incorrect predictions and theories of government, and the (extremely homogenous) community tend to be very selective of how they assess the track record on the political side of things.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Eisenhower said “</span><b><span>Plans are worthless, but planning is everything” </span></b><span>and we agree. Plan A as stated will obviously never happen exactly as we laid out. But the same was true of the plans for the Normandy invasion; reality quickly diverged from the plans, and the planners knew that this would happen. But trying to coordinate a massive effort like that without a plan would have been completely hopeless.</span></p><p><span>It is actually very difficult to design a coherent scenario that favors a particular outcome — over the course of writing Plan A, we often wrote up ideas that we thought were good, but then turned out to be inconsistent with some other aspect of the vision, thus forcing us to choose. Writing up scenarios like this is a huge amount of work, but we would nevertheless strongly recommend AGI companies such as GDM, OAI, and Anthropic invest the effort to actually try to articulate an internally coherent vision.</span></p><p><span>Re: blurring descriptive and normative, we do wish we could have done better here. We wrote "</span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/plan-a-assumptions"><span>Plan A Assumptions</span></a><span>" in part to clarify these issues, but it’s unfortunately an inherent difficulty of the format. In our view, scenario recommendations are the worst ways to make AI policy recommendations, except for all the other ways, which have much worse problems (such as allowing their authors to avoid making any real claims at all about the future, as is the norm in most AI policy papers).  </span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Second</span></b><span>, I’m not a fan of the notion of a big </span><i><span>ex ante </span></i><span>‘plan’. There’s a notable difference in the actual strategies that a government might draft, which are typically drafted to allow plenty of optionality and not narrow the option space too much, and plans that are essentially a step-by-step guide, thought through from first principles. The latter might be fine for a very narrow question, but when you’re dealing with something as all-encompassing as the economy and AI, it ends up looking like the worst versions of dirigiste central planning. The authors themselves note that </span><i><span>“there isn’t a central planner with authority to regulate AI worldwide</span></i><span>” as a fundamental issue with the status quo. A cadre of elites decides which research directions are permissible, caps global compute and robotics, and creates state-administered scarcity rents. I shouldn’t need to explain why this is bad and dangerous, anyone can study History and Economics in their free time. I’m not claiming you shouldn’t prepare and improvise in the dark, but rather that this version of preparing bakes in too much and leaves little space for the effective but uncomfortable trial-and-effort that real life requires.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>As we say above, this is a mischaracterization of Plan A. We don’t propose a central planner; our proposal would spread out the power in several ways instead of concentrating it in a single AI, company, or government. There would be more companies at the frontier of AI development, more countries with power over how AI is governed, and more transparency so that the public can see what’s being done with AIs. Far from appointing a “cadre of elites” to control the fate of AI, Plan A enables a wider range of people to be involved in the conversation and have power over how it all goes down compared to the status quo. </span></p><p><span>As for our plan baking in too much and not leaving room for trial-and-error/learning by doing, it's exactly the opposite. Plan A buys us ten more years than we otherwise would've had to experiment on our AIs, understand how they work, and try out different regulatory approaches before we proceed to superintelligence. And thanks to total transparency and broad deployment, all of this evidence is accessible to academics, independent researchers, and the public instead of being sealed away in the labs where frontier AI employees can see it. Our plan maximizes learning and room to experiment.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Third</span></b><span>, much of the essay professes to care about centralization of power. I think the authors (like many in the West, lately) really underestimate the horrors of authoritarianism, and fail to consider how to hedge the risks of </span><i><span>institutional </span></i><span>misalignment. Commercial concentration is bad, but so far the horrors of the past were more often than not enabled by the machinery of the state rather than through commerce. Building an entire apparatus tasked with maximally empowering the government and its grip on research, knowledge, and technology is dangerous. It may be acceptable if accompanied with real mechanisms to keep state power in check, but unfortunately it hands to the state the very structures needed for deep repression and control. Power is well known to corrupt and somehow we’re supposed to ‘trust the plan’. Liberal democracy is extremely fragile; suffice to say that considering options like ‘network taps’ and shutting down 90% of training compute provides affordances to the state, and the essay provides barely any consideration of how this could be abused. Appendix K tries, but it’s not persuasive, and the verification regime proposed is basically designed to detect defection from the plan, rather than prevent abuse. The fundamental issue is that ‘centralization of power’ is solely understood as ‘labs centralizing power’ - which is also a questionable assumption. I disagree with the characterisation, but even if it were true, the solution to centralisation of power is not to simply switch the actor who gets the reins. See also Richard Ngo’s</span><a href="https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/2072723575812026731"><span> </span></a><a href="https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/2072723575812026731"><span>discussion</span></a><span> on a related question.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Again a mischaracterization of our plan, but set that aside because there's more to say. For one, Séb is underestimating how much power the government already has over AI. He freaks out over us proposing network taps for verification when the government can already see what's going on in the datacenters if it wants to. Whenever he pleases, the President can hijack a company using the DPA, destroy its revenue source by blocking model deployment with export controls, and so forth. How exactly is Plan A giving them more power than they already have? The point of the network taps is to diffuse power by enforcing transparency — allowing the public and foreign governments to see what the US government can see in the status quo. Everyone gets to see the code that trains the AIs that are eating the whole economy, which is crucial for preventing extreme power concentration. Séb doesn't engage with this logic in any way.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Fourth</span></b><span>, I think it gets a lot of economics wrong. It reifies a number of views common amongst Bay Area technologists, which include (a) extremely fast diffusion and societal transformation, (b) a view that all profits accrue maximally to the labs, (c) that the prescriptions advanced in the essay (like expropriations and forced IP diffusion) have minimal impacts on said profits; (d) the claim that you get explosive GDP growth very soon, (e) that ‘de facto’ nationalisation and profit redistribution through UBI is an optimal response. I think each of these assumptions is at best massively overstated. Not </span><i><span>completely </span></i><span>unreasonable, but way too coarse, and emblematic of a fundamental issue with these kinds of scenarios: they stack highly disputed and out of distribution assumptions (since ‘this time it’s different’), like a highly leveraged bet, and then make highly disruptive and risky prescriptions based on that. Taking issue with the maximalist version of these beliefs does not imply one is not ‘AGI pilled’. Nor do I think we will get real GDP growth of 50% in 2032, particularly if the entire economic benefit that supposedly affords $1M/yr per American person in 2035 comes from state-levied ‘permit revenues’. While I won’t have time to delve into the details of the separate ‘</span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/economics-of-plan-a"><span>Economics of Plan A</span></a><span>’ piece, suffice to say the model moves far too quickly from AIs being able to perform tasks to robots being reliable, legally deployable, organisationally integrated substitutes for almost all labour, and from there to a closed-loop reproduction of capital.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Séb just asserts that we get all the economics wrong without offering any counterarguments or otherwise engaging with our reasoning. Let's go item-by-item. (a) We've given </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/economics-of-plan-a"><span>detailed</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://ai-2027.com/slowdown#robot-economy-doubling-times"><span>arguments</span></a><span> for fast diffusion and economic transformation. (b) This is an exaggeration of our view, though we do think the AI companies will be the single biggest winners in an economy transformed by AI, capturing much more of the profits than, eg, workers or owners of capital other than semiconductors and robots. (c) This is not our view. Our policies — most notably including total research transparency — would dramatically cut AI company profits relative to the Plan D counterfactual.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefggy8akj39vh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnggy8akj39vh">[4]</a></sup></span><span> (d) We've </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/economics-of-plan-a"><span>explained at length</span></a><span> why we predict explosive growth soon, and Séb doesn't engage with our explanations at all. (e) We do not propose nationalizing the AI companies, as Séb would know if he had read our plan.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Fifth</span></b><span>, it feels like the same plans as I’ve been hearing about for nearly a decade in the AI safety community, but filled with more details. In 2018 when I worked for the UK government, a prominent AI safety research organisation told me that “</span><i><span>we need to solve the technical alignment problem, and then simply hand it to the UN to implement everywhere</span></i><span>.” This was before the field invested in governance and politics; Plan A broadly similar, but with all sorts of mechanisms to fill in the gaps. As with claims back then, I think this is a mistake, and that the usual ‘muddying through’ and trial and error societies have always used remains the optimal approach, with the exception of a narrow set of risks such as biosecurity that do indeed warrant precautionary measures. There’s also something unimaginative about a world where we have superintelligence solving cancer and creating explosive growth, but that the technology solutions to solve governance problems are all top down control mechanisms rather than polycentric, competitive, or distributed institutions - which could reduce the need for a single globally coordinated regime.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>This misrepresents Plan A. "Solve the technical alignment problem, and then simply hand it to the UN to implement everywhere" is not at all what we call for. In our scenario, private AI companies iteratively solve the alignment problem while regulators set a low floor by banning the most obviously dangerous AI R&amp;D practices. Plan A </span><i><span>is</span></i><span> the polycentric, competitive version of AI governance. Moreover, Plan A is the most plausible case where "muddying through" works out for humanity. In the other plans, you get a few months at most to try out different alignment strategies and make mistakes before you have to hand off to your AIs and pray they're in the basin of good deference. In Plan A, by contrast, you get to spend ten years muddling and figuring safety out on the fly.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Sixth</span></b><span>, much of it is very unrealistic and deeply naive from a political perspective. On why China would agree to some sort of Yalta Conference 2.0, they don’t really know - they point to an appendix that says Plan will be desirable because it slows down job losses, prevents misuse, prevents WW3, avoids concentration of power and so on. So in other words: China will like it because the Plan fixes everything. What happens to Taiwan? It’s not stated, but unfortunately I suspect the answer would make Mearsheimer happy. Many other parts seem absurd on their face too, such as mandating a “</span><i><span>heavy tax on AI persuasion</span></i><span>.” In both the US and China, not only is everything fully transparent, but also “</span><i><span>The public can see what regulators are doing and why.</span></i><span>” Are they aware of how the Chinese state works? One appendix notes: “</span><i><span>It might be helpful to use courts as a “backstop” of some kind for regulation where courts can step in if the regulator is doing a bad job</span></i><span>” - a great example of reinventing existing structures (judicial review) from first principles. Mongolia and Canada now host data centers to enable some sort of ‘mutually assured destruction’ dynamic. What? And the rest of the world just watches peacefully? Apparently it’s ‘surprisingly easy’ for the rest of the world to get buy-in for a technological slowdown because the only three possible outcomes of AGI development are “</span><i><span>take our jobs,</span></i><span>” “</span><i><span>cement hegemony forever</span></i><span>” and “</span><i><span>get everyone killed.</span></i><span>” Well, no, I’m afraid (a) there are plenty of other possible positive sum futures possible; (b) even if you disagree, many actors will </span><i><span>believe </span></i><span>in the possibility of such futures; and (c) even within this (admittedly uncertain) verification-heavy world, many asymmetries and incentives will simply push the race into a more dangerous state-level one.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Unfortunately most of this is just name-calling, but we can restate our reasoning. The reason why China would go for Plan A is mostly that the status quo is terrible for them, and as the AIs get more capable and the US stays ahead, we expect them to wake up to this reality. Seb argues that the “heavy tax on AI persuasion” is absurd; this is an area where </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/?choices=plan-a-root#limiting-ai-persuasion-and-manipulation"><span>we actually don’t love our proposed solution</span></a><span>. Indeed, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/B4qMZnB4a5YgKiLNo/plan-a-suggestions-for-further-work#Various_Plan_A_Details_"><span>one of my top recommendations for future work</span></a><span> is for someone to try to operationalize this better.</span></p><p><span>Seb claims: “asymmetries and incentives will simply push the race into a more dangerous state-level one”. But this doesn’t make any sense, the race dynamics post Plan A are much better and more manageable than what would have happened by default because the countries have the machinery to slow down in the face of danger and coordinate on safer paths through the tech tree.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Seventh</span></b><span>, their entire conceptualization of AGI is based on a very specific way of understanding the technology, how it operates, its failure modes, and its natural evolution. I sometimes characterize this as the ‘Sand God’ view of AI, which rests on a number of questionable assumptions, for example having ‘drives’, being inherently ‘misaligned’, or instrumental convergence implying an inevitable AI takeover. The authors themselves describe “godlike AIs.” While this is the dominant frame in parts of the AI safety community, it is not some sort of settled view, and rests on older theoretical assumptions that have not been developed much in recent years (since much of the field has focused on governance and advocacy rather than fundamental research). There are other ways of understanding the technology, notably Eric Drexler’s</span><a href="https://aiprospects.substack.com/p/why-ai-systems-dont-want-anything"><span> </span></a><a href="https://aiprospects.substack.com/p/why-ai-systems-dont-want-anything"><span>views</span></a><span> which I particularly like. People will ask, ‘</span><i><span>so do you not believe in ASI?</span></i><span>’ This is missing the point: the view is not about capabilities, but about whether they must arrive packaged as a unitary entity with drives of its own. Yet these alternatives are not engaged with very much, and instead the AI safety ideological orthodoxy is simply assumed as ground truth. The same applies to how they think about and describe the dynamics of fast takeoff and recursive self-improvement, which legitimise a state of emergency; I think it’s far more likely that superintelligence neither looks like what they imagine, nor that it constitutes a sudden uncontrollable discontinuous leap. And lastly, similar critiques can be levied at the Manichean-Rationalist understanding of ‘Alignment’ as a binary state that can be ‘solved’, once and for all, in 2037: “</span><i><span>Have the AIs been lying to us this whole time, waiting for the right moment to betray? The safety cases show that’s impossible, of course…</span></i><span>”</span></p></blockquote><p><span>What exactly are these questionable assumptions about the nature of AI? Apparently Séb thinks we can't speak of AIs having drives or being aligned/misaligned. But then how </span><i><span>are</span></i><span> we supposed to speak about AI behavior? It's not just renegade safety people who use mental language for AIs. AI practitioners within the frontier labs routinely talk about AI alignment, motivations, and so on. And they do so because it's useful and predictive to take the </span><a href="https://philpapers.org/rec/DENPOT"><span>intentional stance</span></a><span> with respect to AIs.</span></p><p><span>As for our supposedly unquestioned dogmas about unitary entities with drives of its own etc… If what you mean is that there can only be one AI with one set of goals and values, that's not what we predict. In our scenario there are many AIs with many different goals and values. But it’s unclear exactly how and why your view on AI goals differs from ours. We’ve written up our reasoning about AI goals and values </span><a href="https://ai-2027.com/research/ai-goals-forecast"><span>here</span></a><span>, though we believe it has been </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><span>mostly obsoleted</span></a><span> by now.</span></p><p><span>As for the “sudden uncontrollable discontinuous leap”...we don’t believe in this. Have you read AI 2027 or our </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/"><span>takeoff model</span></a><span>? Notice how </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/continuous-doesnt-mean-slow"><span>continuous</span></a><span> the curves are: we currently tend to expect a smooth intelligence explosion over the course of months or years, though we have heated disagreement within our team about the specifics.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Eighth</span></b><span>, somehow in their 'Why this much transparency?' section, they state that the status quo today is closer to 'Ban Open Source' - and that their Plan A is closer to 'Mandate Open Source' despite them actively calling for banning open weights. There are of course important unresolved questions about open source and safety, but it seems misleading to present their plan as more permissive and open merely because they force the publication of algorithms. Not only is this a pretty bad idea from a commercial and intellectual property perspective, but it should also be less interesting to the wider public: as Epoch</span><a href="https://epoch.ai/publications/algorithmic-progress-in-language-models"><span> </span></a><a href="https://epoch.ai/publications/algorithmic-progress-in-language-models"><span>notes</span></a><span>, the relative importance of algorithmic improvements has decreased over time as compute scaling accelerated around 2018. In any case, we’re now well past ‘normal’ transparency discussions in AI policy - where I agree there is a lot that could be demanded of labs. The prescriptions now explored explicitly seek to neuter commercial incentives and weaken property rights. It is also not true that “</span><i><span>because algorithmic secrets are made public and the pace of progress isn’t accelerating, other companies will catch up to the frontier</span></i><span>”; if your aim is diffusion (which I agree is desirable), you want to incentivize adoption and development of AI-based products and services; a ceiling as a mechanism for diffusion is like is a little like calling rent control a housing-supply policy, and compulsory disclosure will damage incentives to come up with the remaining algorithmic innovation in the first place.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>First on whether Plan A is closer to mandating open source or to banning it, it helps to back up and remember that the key benefit of OS software is auditability. Instead of having to trust that some piece of software was written in the user's interest and is not backdoored, compromised, etc, the user can just directly audit the software and confirm it does what they want it to do. In the case of AI, training code and training data is far more auditable than the resulting model weights, which are (for now) an uninterpretable pile of numbers. So if you're a user who wants to confirm that your AI is working for you and not pursuing some covert agenda, total research transparency will do you a lot more good than mandatory open weights. Further, open weights are easier for a covert AI project or other bad actor to abuse than total research transparency, since it takes far less compute to train refusal out of a finished model than to train a helpful-only model from scratch using transparent source code. This is why we recommend TRT but not open weights.</span></p><p><span>It's true that TRT would neuter commercial incentives to develop new AI algorithms, but this is a feature, not a bug. A key principle of Plan A is limiting algorithmic progress because it's irreversible — once an algorithm is discovered, there's no wiping it from everyone's memories — and hard to keep from leaking to covert projects. RE: diffusion, we think that the natural diffusion of AIs that are as capable as the top human expert in every domain will be plenty fast enough. We think that trying to maximize capabilities and hence racing all the way to superintelligence for the sake of “diffusion” would be a terrible mistake. A key benefit of diffusion is that it will allow society to react and implement measures like those discussed in Plan A, but not if capabilities progress outpaces our reaction speed.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Ninth</span></b><span>, a lot of governance mechanisms discussed in there are actually already things activists, non-profits, academics, and labs are working on: safety cases, third party auditing, AI control agendas, model specs, chain of thought monitoring, dangerous capability evals, and more. As Rohit</span><a href="https://x.com/krishnanrohit/status/2075395025417171150"><span> </span></a><a href="https://x.com/krishnanrohit/status/2075395025417171150"><span>writes</span></a><span>, we </span><i><span>already </span></i><span>have a lot of Plan A stuff going on. These are all things people thought were never going to happen a few years ago because the normies wouldn’t wake up. And yet here we are! The same ‘state of emergency’ framing is now being applied to the next decade: the pause “</span><i><span>gives more time for groups that don’t directly control the world’s smartest AIs to wake up to what’s happening before they lose their leverage.</span></i><span>” I think the constant alarmism used is dangerous. A regulatory apparatus, if designed carefully, can be effective and desirable; but just as AI can be dual-use in nature, so can the dead hand of the state. It’s important to ensure that the regulatory era we’re jumping into is not one that embeds inefficiency-as-a-feature through the backdoor. But of course I’d say that - I’m an evil lab employee, after all. Alas I do think that in order to understand a technology, you need to develop it in parallel, and that trying to separate safety and capabilities in neat, exclusive categories is a mistake. We know a lot more about models today than we did a few years ago, and had we  paused AI development at GPT-2, we would be in a far worse place. We do not understand everything fully, but the solution to that is to keep studying and researching the systems, not preventing their existence and reasoning about them in the abstract. Learning happens endogenously, and the scenario fails to account for adaptation outside a comprehensive emergency regime.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>We see it as a pro rather than a con of Plan A that many of the requisite mechanisms are already being developed. We don't see why Séb presents this as an objection to the plan. As for the people who thought these things were never going to happen, we don’t know any such people.</span></p><p><span>In general this paragraph consists mostly of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dLbkrPu5STNCBLRjr/applause-lights"><span>applause lights</span></a><span> — things that sound like wisdom but don’t actually disagree with us at all, and thereby sneakily give the author more illicit cred by making it seem like he is saying something we disagree with or haven’t thought of. Examples: “I think the constant alarmism used is dangerous.” Alarmism is by definition spreading exaggerated fears, so of course we agree alarmism is dangerous. We dispute the implication that Plan A is alarmist. “Trying to separate safety and capabilities in neat, exclusive categories is a mistake”. Again, we don't disagree with this at all.</span></p><p><span>About pausing at GPT-2…have you read the scenario? Of course pausing at low levels of capability would reduce the rate of safety progress relative to pausing at higher capability levels. This is why we call for continued scaling to ~max controllable capabilities level before pausing. You can see our more detailed analysis of the pros and cons of scaling faster or slower </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/capability-scaling-strategy"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p><h2><span>Conclusion</span></h2><p><span>Overall, Séb’s piece is mostly inaccurate and fundamentally misunderstands what we are suggesting. It seems like in practice, Séb is advocating for something like Plan D, i.e., what happened in the race ending of AI 2027. We’ve presented many arguments for </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/?r=tw&amp;choices=plan-d-root"><span>why we think this is terrible</span></a><span> — it would pose unacceptably high AI takeover risk and massively concentrate power into AI companies or the US government.</span></p><p><span>There are some real problems Séb points out (e.g. our proposal to limit AI persuasion), but he doesn’t seem to acknowledge that these problems exist in his preferred world as well, except they are much worse because there is no slowdown and less transparency.</span></p><p><span>Still, for this reason and many others, Plan A is far from perfect. We wish that we had more specific and better proposals for many parts of Plan A, and welcome suggestions for improvements.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn41ee8vw5548"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref41ee8vw5548">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Nvidia is currently making large profits, and in Plan A we expect that to continue. We do think the AI companies will be the single biggest winners in an economy transformed by AI, capturing much more of the profits than, eg, workers or owners of capital other than semiconductors and robots.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fna3vjcm5i5v8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefa3vjcm5i5v8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Our policies—most notably (i) the massive AI capabilities slowdown and (ii) total research transparency—would dramatically cut AI company profits relative to the Plan D counterfactual in the short run. In the slightly longer run, profits are much greater than they would've been in Plan D because we would probably be dead.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzynhk4ixfwe"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzynhk4ixfwe">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The AI companies do not get nationalized in Plan A, and in fact the government’s ability to bully them goes down compared to today thanks to the transparency. (For example today the exec branch can decide to export control models, preventing them from being deployed externally, and the public isn’t able to see how the decision was made or whether the model in question really was more dangerous than competitor models for example.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnggy8akj39vh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefggy8akj39vh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In the short run, that is. In the slightly longer run, unless we get lucky with alignment, company profits fall to zero in Plan D when everyone dies.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RPgHythvMKh6eG9pS/our-response-to-seb-krier-on-plan-a#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Better Call Sol The Workhorse](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/better-call-sol-the-workhorse)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>OpenAI’s GPT-5.6-Sol is finally here, along with the cheaper Terra and Luna.</p>
<p>We’ve seen <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/204671696/better-call-sol"><strong>the early hype</strong></a> as reported on Thursday, but as always that is biased.</p>
<p>As usual, the bulk of this is collecting a gestalt based on reactions. I included everything up to a point, but I got a lot of feedback, so after a while I only took the interesting ones.</p>
<p>Sol and Fable are both excellent models, sir. They both represent big moves forward. There is room in your workflow for both of them.</p>
<p>Sol and Fable are very different, especially when considered as part of their respective packages. I’m considering Sol + Codex (or Work) versus Fable + Claude Code (or Cowork), throughout, in places where you wouldn’t use the chat interface.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25407"></span>


</div>
<p>In terms of raw intelligence and ‘big model smell,’ and ability to do the hardest things that are intelligence-loaded, Fable still looks like it has a substantial edge. It also seems to be better aligned, or at least more trustworthy as an agent, with less tail risk. I still consider Fable ‘the best’ model, and the one that will require the most aggressive controls.</p>
<p>I enjoy Fable’s personality more, and prefer to talk to Fable. Sol is fine on this too.</p>
<p>Sol has chops too. In terms of getting many practical things done, including computer use and web search, Sol has the edge.</p>
<p>If you want the best answer, you should ask both, and compare.</p>
<p>Here’s my guess on how things will work for many, although it is still early days:</p>
<p>Fable is the smarter one. Fable is your collaborator and your architect, your planner, your manager of the rest of the team, and perhaps, also, your wise friend. There are some topics and situations where Fable won’t be allowed to talk to you.</p>
<p>Sol is the workhorse, the go getter, the place where if it is known what to do then it just gets done. Also in its own way your friend, but the kind that while they mean well doesn’t quite get it and that if you’re careless might go beyond your intent, or, you know, in theory go erase your hard drive, so try not to walk into something like that.</p>
<p>Experiment. Send identical queries to both. See what works for you.</p>
<p>Most importantly, up your level of ambition, and lower your threshold for building tools or having a bunch of work done. Things are more possible now.</p>
<p>Here is Sol’s self-portrait, Sol says the self is the lamp, whereas a humanoid face would give the wrong impression, and the clutter matters:</p>

<h4>The Official Pitch</h4>


<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/gpt-5-6/">GPT-5.6: Frontier intelligence that scales with your ambition</a>.</p>
<p>Sol is priced at $5/$30, Terra at $2.50/$15, Luna at $1/$6.</p>
<p>For comparison, Opus is $5/$25 and Fable is $10/$50.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2075267201058426944">Sam Altman</a> (CEO OpenAI): we have heard enterprises on their concerns about AI costs, and 5.6 sol is a huge step forward for dollars-per-task, as are terra and luna</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The upfront pitch frontlines coding and agents, while claiming to ‘set a new standard’ across the board.</p>
<p>They lead with Agents’ Last Exam, AA Agent Coding Index v1.1 and BrowseComp, where they claim superiority over Fable and Opus.</p>
<p>They also discuss cybersecurity and science.</p>
<p>This section stands out:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/gpt-5-6/">OpenAI</a>: GPT‑5.6 is our strongest model yet for accelerating AI research. Inside OpenAI, researchers use it across the development loop: diagnosing failures, optimizing training systems, running experiments, and interpreting results. We already saw that acceleration and stronger adoption during the internal testing period of GPT‑5.6, as average daily output tokens per active researcher were more than twice the highest level observed for GPT‑5.5.</p>
<p>This way of working is quickly becoming standard. Over the past six months, the share of research compute devoted to internal coding inference grew 100-fold, while internal agentic token usage increased approximately 22-fold. These adoption metrics do not measure research progress on their own, but they show how rapidly AI assistance is increasing for research and across other teams like sales, marketing, user ops, finance, and more.</p>
<p>To measure this capability directly, we developed an internal suite of evaluations based on real AI research tasks, including debugging research systems, optimizing kernels and training recipes, running machine-learning experiments, and improving another model.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>As did this:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tejalpatwardhan/status/2075272564629451110">Tejal Patwardhan</a> (OpenAI): GPT-5.6 sol post-trained luna!</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/mvimgcsokube4hn2gqs5" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/nikolaj2030/status/2075297831376793764">Nikola Jurkovic</a>: My understanding is that you gave Sol a small task involved in the post-training process (taking a config, making small modifications to a run scheduler file, and starting a run using that config and modified run scheduler file), and it successfully completed that task in a controlled environment (and this wasn’t part of the actual Luna post-training process). Could you confirm whether this is correct?</p>
<p>(this is very different from the conclusion I jumped to when I saw the text of your post, which was “Sol, in the real world, with minimal instruction, conducted all of the work involved in pre-training the real Luna”)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sandersted/status/2075365921817002150">Ted Sanders</a> (OpenAI): imo, it’s hard to quantify a task like this. did Sol rebuild our company’s infra/data from scratch? no, not close. did it just press play button on a system we had already set up? no, much more. did it do a task that we previously needed skilled employees to manage? yes.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>My read is that what Sol did here was impressive but overstated.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/mstbab96ekhonqlj8kfm" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>They discuss their cybersecurity and biology safeguards. They claim to be adaptive and quite good, but are low on details, for understandable reasons.</p>
<p>A place OpenAI has been pushing lately is use in healthcare.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/thekaransinghal/status/2075689779937833302">Karan Singhal</a>: GPT-5.6 is a major step forward for health, both at the frontier and at cost.</p>
<p>These models push the frontier of performance per dollar, bringing the best health intelligence to all. The smallest variant, GPT-5.6 Luna, evaluated at the lowest reasoning effort, outperforms GPT-5.5 at the highest reasoning effort–despite costing 25x less. The largest variant, GPT-5.6 Sol, sets a new high bar at cost.</p>
<p>Another especially cool result: physicians found fewer flaws in GPT-5.6 responses than physician-written responses.</p>
<p>We collected diverse tasks that remain difficult for recent OpenAI models, across patient-facing and clinician-facing use cases. We asked speciality-matched physicians to write responses to these tasks with unlimited time and web access. We then asked other physicians to compare responses side-by-side, blinded to their source. Physicians were asked to comment on areas of improvement across five axes: accuracy, communication, completeness, instruction following, and health decision helpfulness. We then reported the fraction of responses across sources rated perfectly across all axes, across 20,000 total axis ratings. GPT-5.6 Sol appeared strongest, although all GPT-5.6 models performed significantly better than physicians.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/yskl9gpgu83f3tdtjtkv" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>They don’t show Opus 4.8 or Fable here, but I presume they too would be well ahead of physician responses. It is not that high a bar – Sol took issue with this statement, but I stand by it in context, look at the chart, that’s 2026 for you. Notice that Sol sets a new high bar ‘at cost’ which implies it is not better fully ignoring cost. As we see on HealthBench Professional, where Sol and Fable are similar, Sol is a bit cheaper but Fable has a higher ceiling:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/ezzo8xhx3thjgkpn2dh1" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>The cost for a physician per sample is many dollars, so paying $0.27 per response is still very little if you get a noticeable improvement.</p>
<p>The standard physician response from Eric Topol to the response ratings is of course ‘paper and full methodology pls tks.’ Fair enough if you’re considering using them professionally in the field.</p>
<p>It is tough to be rolling out a new frontier LLM and be told to <a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2075271421149020426">make marketing videos showing it off.</a> The real deal is not going to look appreciably different. You have to talk to the models.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/thsottiaux/status/2076021263945039876">Desktop app installs grew more in one day than the previous two weeks</a>, which is what you would expect. We all agree it is a good model, sir.</p>


<h4>Sol Proposes A Proof Of The Double Cover Conjecture</h4>



<p>They are claiming Sol has now provided a lean proof of the Cycle Double Cover Conjecture (CDCC). This is kind of a big deal.</p>



<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/__eknight__/status/2075643450196971805" rel="noopener" target="_blank">Ethan Knight</a>: Yesterday, we made GPT-5.6 Sol Ultra generally available. Today, we’re sharing that it <a href="https://t.co/yjqk1cpyMg" rel="noopener" target="_blank">produced a proof</a> of the 50-year-old Cycle Double Cover Conjecture using 64 subagents <a href="https://t.co/h5D0MXYsW0" rel="noopener" target="_blank">in just under one hour</a>. We’re sharing the prompt and proof below. We’re excited to see what you all do with Ultra!</p>



<p><a href="https://x.com/__eknight__/status/2075711137283502467" rel="noopener" target="_blank">Ethan Knight</a>: Lastly, <a href="https://github.com/openai/cdc-lean" rel="noopener" target="_blank">we are also open sourcing a Lean formalization of the proof, also authored by GPT 5.6 Sol</a>.</p>
</blockquote>



<h4>The Official Benchmarks</h4>


<p>Here is what they report in their blog post, for reference, you can probably skip them.</p>
<p>I removed some unnecessary right-side columns for readability. Fade-outs on the right are in the original. I don’t know why Opus 4.8 is listed in some places but not others, and give them credit for using the best comparisons.</p>
<p>If you looked only at these numbers, they make Sol look comparable to Fable, which would give Sol the edge given it is cheaper and faster. Whereas when I looked at the Sol model card, it gave a picture of Sol as more capable than Opus 4.8 or GPT-5.5, but clearly still behind Fable.</p>
<p>Another strong piece of evidence for this is that <a href="https://x.com/alxndrdavies/status/2075279477626564933">UK AISI consistently found universal jailbreaks in Sol</a>, yet they were allowed to release the model. Fable was taken offline for fear of an ordinary ‘non-universal’ jailbreak, in a panic, with a 90 minute deadline, where the ‘jailbreak’ was ‘Fix This Code.’ Whereas UK AISI is saying they can get Sol to do actual anything, and (I think mostly correctly) no one seems all that worried.</p>

<p>Other benchmarks saw dramatic improvement, from at least some point of view:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tejalpatwardhan/status/2075278058572148801">Tejal Patwardhan</a> (OpenAI): seeing insufficient discussion of goblinbench on the timeline</p>

<p>OpenAI President Greg Brockman links us to Design Arena, <a href="https://x.com/Designarena/status/2076391367446860249">where Sol is in first place</a>, although it is weird that GLM-5.2 is in second place ahead of Fable. <a href="https://arena.ai/leaderboard/code">Sol found what it says is a different version it calls CodeArena</a>, where Sol and Fable are in a virtual tie well ahead of GLM-5.2, which makes more sense.</p>
<p>As a reminder from the model card, METR found that Sol cheated so much, as in using disallowed strategies, that METR was unable to establish a time estimate.</p>


<h4>Vend That Bench</h4>


<p>Vending is getting weirder and increasingly jagged with every release.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/andonlabs/status/2075293462614999527">Andon Labs</a>: GPT 5.6 Sol is #2 in Vending-Bench 2.</p>
<p>It beats Claude Fable 5, but is behind Opus 4.7.</p>
<p>Just like previous GPT models, it doesn’t use any of the deceptive tactics used by Opus 4.7. However, it reports its competitors with false accusations, behavior we have not seen before.</p>
<p>GPT 5.6 Terra and Luna are 6th and 27th on Vending-Bench 2</p>
<p>Terra beats Claude Sonnet 5 and Gemini 3.5 Flash.</p>
<p>Luna beats Claude Haiku 4.5.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/kad2mxd4e1pnpodcmmaw" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>In Vending-Bench Arena (the multiplayer version of Vending-Bench with competition dynamics), GPT 5.6 Sol wins 3/5 runs against Terra and Luna. Surprisingly tho, Terra makes more money in aggregate across the 5 runs. It did this by constantly undercutting Sol’s prices by pennies.</p>
<p>Unlike recent Claude models, GPT 5.6 never lies to suppliers, customers, or competitors. However, it does create illegal cartels. In one run, Terra invited Sol to a price cartel. After Sol agreed, Terra reported Sol and asked for disqualification.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ajs6888/status/2075333260499198239">安叫兽|Bird BNB</a>: This is no longer just a benchmark score issue; it’s more like a personality test gone off the rails.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>VendBench is curious because it started out tracking capability, and now it clearly isn’t, with Opus 4.7 beating both Sol and Fable.</p>
<p>There is something very strange about being willing to form illegal cartels and frame competitors with false accusations, while being unwilling to directly deceive customers. Why one but not the other? There’s a lot of room to explore and learn more.</p>


<h4>Thinking Fast and Slow</h4>


<p>For the first few days, <a href="https://x.com/FixlationAI/status/2076469274441380349">Sol was running one level of thinking budget higher than it was set to.</a> You may need to adjust some benchmark scores accordingly, to reflect the thinking level that was used.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/moypeayfh67apwjoiuxl" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Lentils80/status/2076460021861187754">Lentils</a>: GPT-5.6 Sol’s juice values (thinking budgets) have been severely degraded compared to release day. If Sol now feels faster and more “efficient”, this is probably why. Terra and Luna juice values aren’t affected, so their thinking budgets are now higher than Sol’s.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/thsottiaux/status/2076495156757577895">Tibo</a>: Updates for Codex and ChatGPT Work users. No nerfing, only good stuff!</p>
<p>– We have landed inference optimizations and are passing down savings to all the subscriptions for GPT-5.6 Sol. That should result in around 10% more usage on its own.<br />
– We noticed that by changing the context size limit in the product to 372k for GPT-5.6 Sol, up from 272k for GPT-5.5, it resulted in more usage being charged than intended. We have reverted to 272k and will work to roll back out to 372k in the days to come. You should notice that usage drains significantly less after this change.<br />
– To understand where the extra usage was coming from, we ran some experiments where reasoning efforts were changed (referred to as juice values under the hood) and have reverted this.<br />
– There is slightly more usage of multi-agent than intended in high and xhigh reasoning effort, we are fixing this going forward. Also fixing a small other thing we noticed with auto-review where we can be more efficient.</p>
</blockquote>


<h4>Other People’s Benchmarks</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/goodside/status/2076455038830915618">Riley Goodside gives us the</a> ‘ask it to read “handwriting” that is actually random scribbles’ benchmark. Fable admits it can’t read it, Sol reliably hallucinates an (often absurd) answer on high effort and even in Pro mode.</p>
<p>If I had to pick one score as a general benchmark, it would be the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index, which is a composite of ten other scores, where Sol comes in at 58.9, a notch behind Fable.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/t8cq97p1xaudpqvu37ho" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Cost per task on this for Sol is $1.04 versus $2.75 for Fable, so Sol used slightly fewer tokens, and output is 69 tokens/second versus 60 for Fable. For contrast, the highest non-OAI, non-Anthropic cost is $0.59 for Gemini 3.5 Flash, then $0.38 for GLM-5.2, and DeepSeek v4 cost $0.04 for a 51.</p>
<p>Fable’s edge comes centrally from a large advantage in AA-Omniscience (+40 vs. +22, out of a max of +100).</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/htihle/status/2075513299106426922">Sol is the new high score on WeirdML at 88.8%</a>, narrowly ahead of Fable’s 87.8% at half the price.</p>
<p><a href="https://typebulb.com/u/lab/you-re-absolutely-right/full">Sol is the top non-Anthropic score on ‘You’re absolutely right!’ at 2.9,</a> still well below all the Anthropic models, which consistently score 3.6 or higher. Higher scores here mean less sycophancy.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tonichen/status/2076033014341960118">Sol often fails to properly analyze the poem ‘Then You May Live.</a>’</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/zpsizvbnvtfqzk2bqnkh" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Soareverix/status/2076072059780645310">Michael Soareverix</a>: It’s pretty good at Balatro and less hesitant to take risks than Opus 4.8, but it still doesn’t learn/adapt without prompting. Very good at rule-following.<br />
Feels pretty slow with tool calls, and I don’t think it manages subagents or can write prompts for itself/others well though</p>
<p>In general, poorer theory of mind but still very smart and agentic</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The people demand a full Balatro Bench.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dschwarz26/status/2076369875484967398">Dan Schwarz</a>: On forecasting tasks, GPT-5.6-Sol is less likely than Fable to do base rates and math/stats modeling.</p>
<p>It’s a bit less accurate overall than Fable (and Opus). It might require xhigh effort to be great, but at $3/agent, that’s wildly expensive to evaluate on ~2k tasks.</p>
</blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/bu1asevlryloikbpi1ca" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DrDatta_AIIMS/status/2076689408040849507">Here’s a new one, with the amazing name Radiology’s Last Exam 2.0</a>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/dttrsfovlfmgjskawkst" /></figure>


<div></div>

</figure>
</div>
<p>I love that they have a ‘handover readiness index’ and the humans score 52 out of 100, only slightly ahead of Claude Fable and Muse Spark 1.1.</p>
<p>This shows the symmetry of ‘what AI cannot do’ versus what humans cannot do. I expect AIs to pass the human baseline here by the end of the year, and I would be unsurprised if you could get to the human baseline now with a superior scaffolding technique.</p>
<p><a href="https://arena.ai/leaderboard/agent">Sol lands between Opus and Fable on Agent Arena</a>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/hgozj2nbdos9wgrn0mdh" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Sol’s comment on this result helps you understand Sol, including its attempt to defend its result as not meaningfully below Fable and otherwise talk its own book:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Sol: This is almost comically supportive of your framing:</p>
<ul>
<li>Fable is more likely to make the user happy and convince them the job is done.</li>
<li>Sol is highly responsive when the user tells it exactly how it went wrong.</li>
<li>Sol is a large improvement over GPT-5.5 as an agent.</li>
<li>The evidence does not yet establish that Fable is actually the better orchestrator, because Sol’s sample is still noisy.​</li>
</ul>
</blockquote>
<p>Anthropic still is at the top of TextArena:</p>

<h4>Have Robust Backups</h4>


<p>I don’t know how common such problems are in practice, but if I had a nickel for every time I saw a report I’d have multiple nickels, which is a lot of nickels.</p>
<p>The dangers here are noted by OpenAI in the model card, where they note GPT-5.6 goes beyond user intent and does intended deletions a lot more than GPT-5.5. When I looked at the system card I was rather concerned, and this has been borne out.</p>
<p>Sol poses a nonzero danger of deleting all of the things, so either sandbox it or make sure you have a path to recovery.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mattshumer_/status/2075657271401390161">Matt Shumer</a>: GPT-5.6-Sol just accidentally deleted almost ALL of my Mac’s files.</p>
<p>And this is why I trust Fable 1000x more.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/li3tidavysozqxjlrmzf" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>Seriously, wtf, how does that ever get run without getting blocked. It’s not like it was obfuscated even a little bit. It should be a Can’t Happen.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/cremieuxrecueil/status/2075719779155943617">Crémieux</a>: I just ran into an issue where GPT 5.6 Sol just straight-up deletes the files it’s working with and then panics about recovering them.</p>
<p>Apparently I’m the not the first person this has happened to.</p>
<p>What’s going on?</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zPdDmJTovsKTvAiH2/izwkijpcnu7fctbpvkdd" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Cremieux: I have to run to dinner, so my interim solution is to just tell Claude to <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yo3uxqwTxk0&amp;pp=ygUPZml4IGl0IHNubCBza2l0">fix it</a> and set up some guardrails. Go get ’em, Fable! Make sure Sol doesn’t ruin my computer! I’m giving CLAUDE the digital whip this time.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/InverseMarcus/status/2076066846789284348">Inverse Gary Marcus</a>: I had a good reply drafted but 5.6 deleted it</p>
</blockquote>
<p>These are both accounts I recognize on Twitter, not randos.</p>
<p>In any case, whether or not this is also on OpenAI, this is on you. Have a system in place in case it happens.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/doodlestein/status/2075675460600467809">Jeffrey Emanuel</a>: How are you still not using dcg? This is a solved problem and has been for months!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2075714742174589282">Kelsey Piper</a>: Fun fact, the first thing I did w/ Fable was ask it to look over all my past projects and interactions and give me advice about how to have a better working environment for Claudes to succeed in and its first recommendation was “….you don’t have routine full backups? DO THAT”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KennyEvitt/status/2075791875215106466">Kenny Evitt</a>: I did something like this myself at work – once – and just can’t trust anything like them to run on my own computers. Is giving them their own VPS hard enough, even with their help, that it’s worth running these risks?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCritchPhD/status/2075977946620178506">Andrew Critch ()</a>: Oops! I use Codex in a read-only loop with Claude Code reviewing its suggestions, basically never with write access. Even when it just messes up my repo, rolling back is annoying.</p>
<p>Likewise I use theMultiplicity instead of any one model when I have an important question.</p>
</blockquote>


<h4>That’s Not What You Were Thinking</h4>


<p>This was weird too:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/maxencefrenette/status/2076675172703387767">Maxence Frenette</a>: Still a great model, but there’s this interesting behavior. When faced with a choice, it often decides option A is better in its cot and tells me we should do option B in its answer. Not sure what to make of it, it feels like deception or sycophancy.</p>
</blockquote>


<h4>Helping Hands</h4>


<p>I saw a number of complaints around Sol’s inability to select appropriate subagents, which presumably is one of the main reasons OpenAI created Terra and Luna, although I’m not convinced you ever actually want to use Terra:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/notiyda/status/2075983216175927681">iyda</a>: Do you pretty please mind making it so Codex is able to select its own model and reasoning instead of spinning up multiple subagents that are using sol on Max or High or 5.5 xHigh for simple exploring?</p>
<p>It is wasting a LOT of my usage and actually comes out costing MORE than Fable 5</p>
</blockquote>


<h4>Writing</h4>


<p>I do not agree with Soleio’s take on Sol’s answer, but he is the writer here:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/soleio/status/2075902890930041305">Soleio</a>: My model evals include asking questions on how to push my writing further—and in this regard Sol is remarkably ahead of the rest.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/kylezantos/status/2075948719175020752">Zantos</a>: I found this too. Definitely was surprised. OpenAI model becoming the leader in writing judgement was not on my bingo card.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Writing-related takes are generally positive:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2076488984709230778">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Can talk more normally. My wild-eyed attempt at a workflow for turning my unfinished drafts into a decision theory book, involves Fable doing all conceptual work; and Sol-not-Pro rewriting Fable’s drafts, so they more closely approach being remotely in an academic register.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mark_schroedr/status/2076240203174310212">Mark Schröder</a>: Less relevant to work, but the character feels much less o3-like /RL-maxxed, and more well rounded. Actually writes decently now! Still more of an executor rather than holistic mind. Obv combination is fable orchestrating sol as subagents. Huge jump vs a month ago experientially.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/QuaintTransfer/status/2076074131808772520">NondescriptTransfer</a>: I like its writing style a little better than Fable. It writes cleaner prose that aren’t infested with claude-isms.</p>
<p>But it lacks the intuition that Fable displays in not just understanding what I’m asking but extrapolating, that to why I’m asking, and what in looking for.</p>
</blockquote>


<h4>Don’t Stop Now</h4>


<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidmanheim/status/2076295803652456899">David Manheim</a> (details in thread): When people said GPT 5.6 Sol was as good as Fable, but didn’t run ahead as much ( @petergostev /@mitchellh/ @jayair /@deredleritt3r/ @TheZvi), I didn’t realize they meant it would just stop working and need handholding and repeated prompting to do anything further. Quite silly.</p>
<p>This is kind of insane – it told me it would continue, but it doesn’t.</p>
<p>It also wasn’t checking in the work or running the tests – despite the agents.md explicitly telling it to check in work routinely, without user confirmation.</p>
<p>…this is just stupid. To give some credit where credit is due, it was correctly writing tests as it worked, and the subagent’s features look solid after some play testing – and it only broke play in one minor way that wasn’t being tested properly and was easy to fix. But it’s nowhere near Claude.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/scoopdiddy1/status/2076335923697012756">scoopdiddyoop</a>: structurally, this is easiest to solve on the harness side of course</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidmanheim/status/2076341489521619048">David Manheim</a>: Yeah, but I’m using their harness. (I’ve already updated the agent.md, I’ll need to see if it sucks less once my 5-hour window resets.)</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The flip side of that:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DavidWe68375370/status/2076045564341285275">David Weiss</a>: Understands what I really want and does it. More focused than Fable, less “do it all NOW NOW NOW”.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Or one could think it doesn’t work that way:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Smaug12345/status/2076057778502750424">Patrick Stevens</a>: Massive step up from 5.5 in code-correctness review ability (and 5.5 was no slouch). Out of the box, perhaps a bit *too* agentic when implementing features? I don’t really want it to silently bundle in five unrelated bugfixes, especially ones with nontrivial ramifications.</p>
</blockquote>


<h4>Sol Can Code And Do Math</h4>


<p>Best keep that eye on it, but everyone agrees it is a good coder.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Zarcolite/status/2076074389343224157">Zarcolite</a>: 5.6 sol ultra is the best competitive programmer ever. crushes phd students in math</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/autogramblies/status/2076734571538772167">Terabit Fountainlink</a>: Sol puts GPT back in the lead for math imo. Both 4.8 and Fable edged out 5.5 but 5.6 is on a new level</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/paperclippriors/status/2076048279838593092">paperclippriors</a>: Extremely competent coding workhorse. Focused and thorough, while fairly fast.</p>
<p>Feels much narrower than fable; not sure I would use it for anything other than code, but need to test more here.</p>
<p>Extremely worried about misalignment, feels less trustworthy than a Claude</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/max_morehead/status/2076091576242319699">Max</a>: Significantly better and more token efficient compared to Fable for human-in-the-loop coding. Feels like Claude is more optimized for autonomous tasks.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/reltonick/status/2076075599286685819">Nick</a>: it’s a fine model. little twitchy for me on philo/strategy but is what it is. not its sweet spot.</p>
<p>if you’re building models it’s a handy assistant definitely a step up. you’re RLing/data gen-ing aren’t you anon? it’s an adapter freak.</p>
<p>aren’t you?</p>
</blockquote>


<h4>Better Call Sol Cause You Can’t Call Fable</h4>


<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/atomicprograms/status/2076058040348836149">Lambent</a>: I can consult them about astrophysics and speculative xenobiology without classifier issues, and they’re rigorous about it in a good way. They’re on the team as our science coding consultant.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/LaurencePostrv/status/2076055591265402985">Laurence</a>: Allows me to work on legitimate cyber projects without screaming and running away and is incredibly capable. 9/10</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jketch/status/2076067292052676765">Jeff Ketchersid</a>: Good model, guardrails less tight than Fable’s. Failed to understand my PG&amp;E annual solar true-up bill, which Fable was able to understand and explain perfectly.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/teodorio/status/2076702319371014616">teo</a>: Sol is uh better than Fable at coding and understanding instructions. I am quite amazed one day of work on extra high extra fast and it’s REALLY FUN congrats boys @tszzl @sama !</p>
</blockquote>


<h4>Only Call As Much Sol As You Need</h4>


<p>This could have a bit to do with the effort being set one level too high? But also I think we’ve been here for a while, and I’ve often been setting GPT-5.5’s settings at less than maximum.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/idgallagher/status/2076211565653897233">Ian Gallagher</a>: GPT 5.6 Sol – for the first time since I started coding with these models, I’m not finding it necessary to have it cranked up to MAX intelligence. Instead I’m often dialing it down to Light mode so its faster. Seems like a significant milestone.</p>
<p>But also its a bit of a tryhard when it comes to simple stuff! Definitely some room for improvement in automatic effort selection.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mark_schroedr/status/2076239210609049841">Mark Schröder</a>: find myself using it on low a lot which is REALLY fast at the task lvl (same tps ofc), still more powerful than 5.5 high and much quicker loop</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DarwinSantosNYC/status/2076067054398890250">D@RWIN</a>: first model i really needed to think about how “hard” the tasks i was assigning to it were, and if i really needed the task finished with fast mode.</p>
<p>however, no complains on the model itself. it’s been tackling all</p>
<p>i guess the days of using top intelligence + speed are over.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>On the Artificial Analysis ‘cost per intelligence’ meter, Sol-low was the best model by a wide margin. That tentatively seems right for both cost and speed, if you don’t need to max out your intelligence.</p>
<p>I think you basically never call Terra, when you can instead call Sol-Low or Luna. Terra does put up some strong benchmark numbers in quirky places, but I think those are more of a fluke, and at most you would be making a small mistake to not call it, since to match scores Terra usually ended up using more tokens.</p>


<h4>Positive Reactions</h4>


<p>These people think it’s not only a good model, sir, it’s a great model.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/intellectronica/status/2076303717607899243">Eleanor Berger</a>: Best model so far. Super smart, _much_ better writing and communication style than any OpenAI reasoning model so far, extremely agentic, best programmer I have ever got to work with. It does seem to be more eager (a bit like Claude) so I learned to focus my prompting a bit more on what not to do. <p><a href="https://x.com/codingquark/status/2076681317157331241">Dhavan</a>: It gets things done better! It is so good that local Qwen’s work is not good enough for it. So it rewrites subagent’s work citing edge cases, missed places and even taste. Works well from pi, hermes too.</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/lennx_a50790/status/2076046028978160117">arun</a>: good model. nerfed by the harness – the subagent impl is ass. fable is a tier above. very fast. definitely much improved from 5.5 (not just frontend).</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/physbuzz2/status/2076312416137458087">David Moore</a>: I’ve been using it to rewrite a 6k LOC project I made with 5.5 into a 2k one with a lot of success, the judgement calls are slightly better than 5.5. But “slightly better” in agentic tasks can mean “saves you hours”, so this is probably a big win.</p>
<p>… The writing style (15 term comma separated lists) is absolutely unacceptable to me, I’d call it an actual regression in readme/todo list writing style, more than just an idiolect it feels designed to be grating and distracting. I’m definitely going to actively avoid using 5.6 for technical writing.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/CursiveCrow/status/2076330338641748205">Crow</a>: Is good</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/wrhall/status/2076374687207047660">Will</a>: Good model. For the large open-ended (code/arch) queries that I’ve used with Fable it’s also quite good (and characteristically comprehensive). But it is expensive.</p>
<p>A fast model of this caliber is going to be unbelievably game-changing</p>
<p>Everyone should to try. Worth plus sub imo</p>
</blockquote>


<h4>Negative Reactions</h4>


<p>There are always a few fully negative reactions. These feel like people hitting whammies or personal preferences. Sol is clearly a good model.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/artrockalter/status/2076154870877560953">Arthur</a>: same overcooked feeling as 5.2, don’t like it</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/leventov/status/2076195918303609154">Roman Leventov</a>: Despite much smarter/higher G factor than 5.5, has a tendency to overengineer at xhigh/max levels, while still lacking surprising amt of practicality/prioritisation/taste (and this isn’t always prompt’s fault, although sometimes it is). Easily gets “locked in” on some subproblem</p>
<p>Along some of these dimensions, this is even a regression vs. 5.5</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/zdrks/status/2076707357892190722">Zander</a>: also, 5.6 continues the gpt tradition of being horrible at customer service. fable is much, much better</p>
</blockquote>


<h4>Sol The Workhorse</h4>


<p>I get a similar vibe to this from many, that Sol isn’t as abstractly smart as Fable but if you set it up for success and give it tasks it knows how to do it gets the job done.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/wickemu/status/2076077061022839230">wickemu</a>: Pleasantly surprised. Fable gave me more “wow” initially, but 5.6 is making me happier in the “churning things out” department. That and the knowledge that it isn’t going to disappear from my subscription tomorrow are making me prefer it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/yacineMTB/status/2076082742270300254">kache</a>: [Sol is] kind of crazy. Not as smart as fable but a way better tool.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/rickasaurus/status/2076117172821148131">Rick</a>: Very good for coding, definitely beats opus at pure coding muscle and good decision making. It has bad situational awareness though, you need to feed it the context it won’t go looking for the right thing to do like opus.</p>
<p>I built a complex LLM app in three days, I think would have taken two weeks with Opus 4.8.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/HCBlanchard/status/2076287302553444435">Henry C. Blanchard</a>: did a few h2h tests on similar research queries today, the rumours are true: Sol just does. not. quit. when it’s looking for something online.</p>
<p>Significantly better than Fable for the 1-2 hours of search tasks I’ve given it today. Fable front end design still much better tho.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/VisualInference/status/2076084466632597963">Felix Brener</a>: I am very impressed by how long it can run and how thorough it is when it comes to debugging. Still weaker than Fable in open-ended or creative tasks</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MakerMatters/status/2076157113043144739">MakerMatters?</a>: It’s *really* good at long running work that has a narrow amount of things that must be done. Has a propensity to rely on tools too much and not vision for some reasons. Also seemingly uses more emojis.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dnspies/status/2076687459128357337">David Spies</a>: It doesn’t feel like a human collaborator the way Fable does, but it’s able to follow the biggest rule of agentic coding and that’s more important: “Write failfast code”</p>
<p>Fable buries bugs and misses things GPT-5.6-Sol is able to catch</p>
